Conventional section names\n    64\t\n    65\tSection names are navigation, not titles. A reader scanning the contents must\n    66\tknow what is in each section *without reading it*.\n    67\t\n    68\t- Test: could this table of contents belong to any paper in the field? If a\n    69\t  heading names a concept the paper itself invented, it tells the reader\n    70\t  nothing until they have already read the section.\n    71\t- Fix: use the names the field uses \u2014 Introduction, Related Work,\n    72\t  Preliminaries, Method, Experiments, Results, Analysis, Discussion,\n    73\t  Limitations, Conclusion. Put the invented name in the section's first\n    74\t  sentence, or in a subsection heading underneath the conventional one.\n    75\t- Legitimate variants exist (\"Discussion and Related Work\" when related work\n    76\t  sits at the end). The bar is that the name says what kind of content follows.\n    77\t\n    78\t## 5. Related work, searched with the *final* vocabulary\n    79\t\n    80\tBy the end of the draft the work has a name, a metric, and a problem statement\n    81\tthat the project did not have when it started. The literature search that was\n    82\trun at the beginning could not have used any of them.\n    83\t\n    84\t- Fix: run at least one more search now, using the draft's own final terms \u2014\n    85\t  the contribution's name, the metric's name, the exact problem statement, and\n    86\t  the nearest baseline's name. Fetch real BibTeX (see `SKILL.md`) and cite what\n    87\t  comes back.\n    88\t- Also check the reference lists of the two or three closest papers already\n    89\t  cited; the nearest neighbour is very often cited by one of them.\n    90\t- An uncited close prior work is among the most common reasons a paper is\n    91\t  rejected, and it is entirely preventable at this point.\n    92\t\n    93\t## 6. Figure 1 carries the main idea\n    94\t\n    95\tThe first figure is the one every reader looks at, often before reading a word.\n    96\tIt must answer \"what is this work?\".\n    97\t\n    98\t- Test: shown only Figure 1 and its caption, could a reader say what the paper\n    99\t  proposes or studies?\n   100\t- Fix: Figure 1 shows the system, method, or central concept \u2014 not one narrow\n   101\t  comparison and not a secondary improvement, however strong that result is. If\n   102\t  the current first figure is a specific result, move it into Results and\n   103\t  promote (or specify) an overview figure in its place. Its marker belongs near\n   104\t  the end of the Introduction.\n   105\t- A correct figure in the wrong slot is still the wrong Figure 1.\n   106\t\n   107\t## 7. Report the whole study, not only the highlights\n   108\t\n   109\tIf the work covers N of something \u2014 metrics, models, datasets, configurations,\n   110\tseeds \u2014 then all N must be visible somewhere the reader can check them.\n   111\t\n   112\t- Test: state N explicitly, from the artifacts rather than from the draft. Now\n   113\t  find where all N appear. \"We evaluate 53 metrics\" followed by a figure\n   114\t  showing eight is a gap the reader will assume was chosen to flatter.\n   115\t- Fix: add the complete view \u2014 a full figure, or a complete table, in the body\n   116\t  or an appendix. Highlighting a subset in the main text is good writing;\n   117\t  showing *only* that subset is not.\n   118\t- The same applies to negative and null results from the study. They belong in\n   119\t  the paper.\n   120\t\n   121\t## 8. No implementation-internal references in the prose\n   122\t\n   123\tThe paper describes the work; the repository holds the implementation. A reader\n   124\tcannot follow a sentence that names a file they cannot see.\n   125\t\n   126\t- Test: search the draft for filenames, module paths, function names, class\n   127\t  names, CLI flags, and variable names from the codebase.\n   128\t- Fix: state the rule, not the code that implements it. Not \"`eligibility.py`\n   129\t  declares E1 as ...\" but \"an item is eligible when ...\". If the pointer is\n   130\t  genuinely useful, it goes in a footnote, an artifact link, or an appendix \u2014\n   131\t  never in a sentence the reader has to parse.\n   132\t- Mathematical notation and algorithm names are not affected by this; they are\n   133\t  the paper's own vocabulary, not the implementation's.\n   134\t\n   135\t## 9. Consistency \u2014 several separate passes, one concern each\n   136\t\n   137\tInconsistency is the defect a first draft is *guaranteed* to have: the paper was\n   138\twritten in pieces, over time, while the results were still moving. A single\n   139\t\"check it's consistent\" sweep finds almost nothing, because each concern needs a\n   140\tdifferent thing held in mind. Run these as **separate passes over the whole\n   141\tdocument**, one per row, and repeat any pass that produced an edit \u2014 a fix in one\n   142\tplace routinely breaks agreement somewhere else.\n   143\t\n   144\t| Pass | What to hold in mind | The failure it catches |\n   145\t|---|---|---|\n   146\t| Claim \u2194 evidence | Every claim in the text | A claim with no figure, table, or number behind it; or one whose evidence shows something weaker than claimed |\n   147\t| Evidence \u2194 claim | Every figure and table | A result presented but never discussed, and the reverse: something described in the text that is never actually shown (see item 7) |\n   148\t| Numbers | One value at a time | The same quantity differing between abstract, text, table, figure, and caption |\n   149\t| Citations \u2014 placement | Each `[n]` in context | A reference attached to a claim it does not support, or supporting a claim it only mentions in passing |\n   150\t| Citations \u2014 integrity | The bibliography | Cited but not listed; listed but never cited; the same work under two entries; a fabricated or unverified entry |\n   151\t| Terminology | One term at a time | The same concept under two names, or one name used for two concepts |\n   152\t| Notation | Each symbol | A symbol reused with a second meaning, or used before it is defined |\n   153\t| Cross-references | Each \"Section/Figure/Table N\" | A pointer to the wrong item, or to one that no longer exists |\n   154\t| Section name \u2194 content | Each heading, then its section | A heading that no longer describes what ended up under it after material was moved (item 3 moves material; this pass re-checks the names afterwards) |\n   155\t| Tense and voice | Section by section | Method in past tense in one place and present in another; person switching mid-paper |\n   156\t\n   157\tFor the citation passes specifically: check what each cited work actually says\n   158\tbefore trusting its placement. A citation that is real, correctly formatted, and\n   159\tattached to the wrong sentence is worse than a missing one \u2014 it is a factual\n   160\terror the reader will attribute to carelessness across the whole paper.\n   161\t\n   162\t---\n   163\t\n   164\t## Before finishing\n   165\t\n   166\tConfirm every item above was actually applied to the current text, not to the\n   167\tversion you remember writing. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\",\n     4\t  \"paper_text\": \"# Phonological Density Does Not Predict Dependency Distance Minimization: A Cross-Linguistic Test Across 55 Languages\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is a robust cross-linguistic tendency for syntactically related words to appear in close linear proximity. A recent hypothesis predicted that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories and more complex phonotactics, would exhibit stronger DLM because speakers compensate for parsing difficulty by compressing dependencies. We test this hypothesis across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation (Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance ($R^2 = 0.05$). Word-order typology also fails to predict MDD significantly (ANOVA $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$). We confirm the functional-lexical split: functional dependencies average 2.7 words while lexical dependencies average 3.4 words. The hypothesis that phonological density constrains syntactic optimization is not supported. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\\n\\n## 1. Introduction\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is one of the most replicated empirical regularities in cross-linguistic syntax. Since Liu's [1] foundational study of 15 languages, researchers have documented that heads and dependents tend to appear near each other in linear order, reducing working-memory load during parsing [2, 3]. The effect holds across typologically diverse families and both spoken and written modalities [4].\\n\\nTwo blindspots persist in this literature. First, DLM has always been measured in word-space: distance equals the number of intervening words between head and dependent. This ignores phonological structure. A dependency spanning three monosyllabic words in Japanese occupies far less phonological space than the same word-count dependency in English. Second, no study has asked whether phonological density modulates DLM strength. Languages with larger phoneme inventories and more complex syllable structures face harder parsing problems. If speakers compensate by minimizing syntactic distance, phonologically dense languages should show stronger DLM.\\n\\nWe test this phonotactic constraint hypothesis using Universal Dependencies treebanks from 55 languages spanning 10 families. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and phoneme-space, where distance equals the number of phonemes between head and dependent. We then correlate phonological density\u2014derived from PHOIBLE inventory sizes\u2014with mean dependency distance, controlling for word-order typology.\\n\\nThe results do not support the hypothesis. Phonological density shows a weak positive correlation with MDD ($r = 0.19$), running in the opposite direction from prediction. The effect is not statistically significant ($p = 0.17$). Word order similarly fails to predict MDD. The phoneme-space metric produces larger values (mean 10.6 phonemes) than word-space (mean 3.0 words), confirming that phonological and syntactic distances operate on different scales.\\n\\nThese null findings are informative. They suggest that phonological density and syntactic optimization operate independently, or that the hypothesized compensation mechanism is too weak to detect at the cross-linguistic level. We discuss alternative explanations in Section 5.\\n\\nOur contributions are threefold. First, we introduce a phoneme-space dependency distance metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. Second, we report the largest cross-linguistic test of the phonotactic constraint hypothesis to date, covering 55 languages. Third, we replicate the functional-lexical dependency split [5] and document family-level variance patterns.\\n\\n## 2. Related Work\\n\\nDependency distance minimization was first documented by Liu [1] for 15 languages, establishing MDD as a universal tendency. Futrell et al. [2] extended this to 37 languages using Universal Dependencies, confirming cross-family robustness. Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez [3] showed that reduced dependency distance\u2014the minimum distance over all subsegments\u2014reveals distributed cost structure beyond simple MDD.\\n\\nThe information-theoretic perspective provides a complementary framework. Coup\u00e9 et al. [6] demonstrated that languages encode information at approximately 39 bits per second, with syllable rate and information density compensating for each other. This modulation integrity hypothesis suggests that phonological and syntactic constraints jointly optimize around channel capacity. Our work tests a specific instantiation: whether phonological density modulates syntactic optimization.\\n\\nFunctional versus lexical dependencies form a well-established dichotomy. Gerdes [5] showed that functional dependencies\u2014determiners, case markers, auxiliaries\u2014are universally short because grammar hard-codes their adjacency. Lexical dependencies\u2014subjects, objects, modifiers\u2014are longer and more sensitive to typological variation. Our results confirm this split.\\n\\nCorpus choice affects DLM estimates. Dobrovoljc [7] demonstrated that ordinal rankings of languages by MDD are unstable across treebanks, indicating that corpus composition interacts with distance metrics. Our analysis uses a single standardized treebank per language to minimize this confound, but we acknowledge residual variability.\\n\\nPhonological typology has been linked to syntactic patterns in limited ways. Krielke [8] found diachronic shifts toward intra-phrasal dependencies in scientific German and English, hinting at phonological-density interactions. However, no study has systematically tested whether synchronic phonological density predicts DLM strength.\\n\\n## 3. Method\\n\\n### 3.1 Data\\n\\nWe extracted Universal Dependencies treebanks from the `commul/universal_dependencies` repository on HuggingFace [9]. We selected one configuration per language, preferring treebanks with the largest training splits. This yielded 55 languages across 10 families: Indo-European (28), Uralic (4), Turkic (3), Afroasiatic (4), Sino-Tibetan (4), Austronesian (5), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Tai-Kadai (2), and Austroasiatic (2), plus isolates and unclas<response clipped><NOTE>Due to the max output limit, only part of this file has been shown to you. Mixed-effects modeling with language family as random effect would be preferable but requires larger within-family samples.\\n\\n## 6. Conclusion\\n\\nWe tested whether phonological density predicts dependency distance minimization across 55 languages. The hypothesis predicted stronger DLM in phonologically dense systems. Results showed a weak positive correlation ($r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that failed to reach significance. Phonological density explained only 5% of variance in MDD. Word order also failed to predict MDD significantly.\\n\\nThe null finding does not falsify the phonotactic constraint hypothesis definitively. Plain, professional language\n    17\t\n    18\tWrite the plainest prose the field accepts. Formality is not complexity \u2014 a\n    19\ttop-venue paper reads *simply*; it is the ideas that are hard, not the\n    20\tsentences.\n    21\t\n    22\t- Test: could a competent researcher from a neighbouring subfield follow each\n    23\t  sentence on the first pass, at reading speed?\n    24\t- Fix: replace ornamental vocabulary with the ordinary word. Unpack stacked\n    25\t  noun phrases (\"gradient-based sample-efficiency degradation analysis\").\n    26\t  Split any sentence carrying more than one claim. Cut throat-clearing\n    27\t  (\"It is important to note that\", \"In this work, we importantly\").\n    28\t- Every term of art gets a one-clause definition at first use, including the\n    29\t  names this paper itself invents.\n    30\t\n    31\t## 2. The abstract is prose, not a results table\n    32\t\n    33\tAn abstract dense with numbers cannot be read \u2014 the reader has no axes,\n    34\tbaselines, or units in mind yet, so each number costs them more than it tells\n    35\tthem.\n    36\t\n    37\t- Test: count the numbers in the abstract. More than about three, and it is a\n    38\t  data dump.\n    39\t- Fix: keep only the headline results \u2014 the ones that would appear in a\n    40\t  one-sentence summary of the paper. Move the rest to Results, where they sit\n    41\t  next to the baseline and the axis that make them mean something.\n    42\t- The abstract must state, in words: the problem, what was done, what was\n    43\t  found, and why it matters. A reader who stops after the abstract should be\n    44\t  able to say all four back.\n    45\t\n    46\t## 3. One job per section\n    47\t\n    48\tSections leak in a first draft because the author writes what they know as they\n    49\tthink of it.\n    50\t\n    51\t- Test: read the Introduction alone. Does it contain method detail, result\n    52\t  tables, or a survey of prior work? Those belong to Method, Results, and\n    53\t  Related Work.\n    54\t- Test the reverse direction too, which is the half that gets missed: **no\n    55\t  later section may depend on a definition, formula, symbol, or piece of\n    56\t  notation that appears only in the Introduction.** If Method needs it, it is\n    57\t  defined in Method or in Preliminaries; the Introduction may motivate it, not\n    58\t  own it.\n    59\t- Fix: move the material to the section whose job it is, and leave a\n    60\t  forward-reference (\"we define this formally in Section 3\") if the\n    61\t  Introduction still needs to gesture at it.\n    62\t\n    63\t## 4. Conventional section names\n    64\t\n    65\tSection names are navigation, not titles. A reader scanning the contents must\n    66\tknow what is in each section *without reading it*.\n    67\t\n    68\t- Test: could this table of contents belong to any paper in the field? If a\n    69\t  heading names a concept the paper itself invented, it tells the reader\n    70\t  nothing until they have already read the section.\n    71\t- Fix: use the names the field uses \u2014 Introduction, Related Work,\n    72\t  Preliminaries, Method, Experiments, Results, Analysis, Discussion,\n    73\t  Limitations, Conclusion. Put the invented name in the section's first\n    74\t  sentence, or in a subsection heading underneath the conventional one.\n    75\t- Legitimate variants exist (\"Discussion and Related Work\" when related work\n    76\t  sits at the end). The bar is that the name says what kind of content follows.\n    77\t\n    78\t## 5. Related work, searched with the *final* vocabulary\n    79\t\n    80\tBy the end of the draft the work has a name, a metric, and a problem statement\n    81\tthat the project did not have when it started. The literature search that was\n    82\trun at the beginning could not have used any of them.\n    83\t\n    84\t- Fix: run at least one more search now, using the draft's own final terms \u2014\n    85\t  the contribution's name, the metric's name, the exact problem statement, and\n    86\t  the nearest baseline's name. Fetch real BibTeX (see `SKILL.md`) and cite what\n    87\t  comes back.\n    88\t- Also check the reference lists of the two or three closest papers already\n    89\t  cited; the nearest neighbour is very often cited by one of them.\n    90\t- An uncited close prior work is among the most common reasons a paper is\n    91\t  rejected, and it is entirely preventable at this point.\n    92\t\n    93\t## 6. Figure 1 carries the main idea\n    94\t\n    95\tThe first figure is the one every reader looks at, often before reading a word.\n    96\tIt must answer \"what is this work?\".\n    97\t\n    98\t- Test: shown only Figure 1 and its caption, could a reader say what the paper\n    99\t  proposes or studies?\n   100\t- Fix: Figure 1 shows the system, method, or central concept \u2014 not one narrow\n   101\t  comparison and not a secondary improvement, however strong that result is. If\n   102\t  the current first figure is a specific result, move it into Results and\n   103\t  promote (or specify) an overview figure in its place. Its marker belongs near\n   104\t  the end of the Introduction.\n   105\t- A correct figure in the wrong slot is still the wrong Figure 1.\n   106\t\n   107\t## 7. Report the whole study, not only the highlights\n   108\t\n   109\tIf the work covers N of something \u2014 metrics, models, datasets, configurations,\n   110\tseeds \u2014 then all N must be visible somewhere the reader can check them.\n   111\t\n   112\t- Test: state N explicitly, from the artifacts rather than from the draft. Now\n   113\t  find where all N appear. \"We evaluate 53 metrics\" followed by a figure\n   114\t  showing eight is a gap the reader will assume was chosen to flatter.\n   115\t- Fix: add the complete view \u2014 a full figure, or a complete table, in the body\n   116\t  or an appendix. Highlighting a subset in the main text is good writing;\n   117\t  showing *only* that subset is not.\n   118\t- The same applies to negative and null results from the study. They belong in\n   119\t  the paper.\n   120\t\n   121\t## 8. No implementation-internal references in the prose\n   122\t\n   123\tThe paper describes the work; the repository holds the implementation. A reader\n   124\tcannot follow a sentence that names a file they cannot see.\n   125\t\n   126\t- Test: search the draft for filenames, module paths, function names, class\n   127\t  names, CLI flags, and variable names from the codebase.\n   128\t- Fix: state the rule, not the code that implements it. Not \"`eligibility.py`\n   129\t  declares E1 as ...\" but \"an item is eligible when ...\". If the pointer is\n   130\t  genuinely useful, it goes in a footnote, an artifact link, or an appendix \u2014\n   131\t  never in a sentence the reader has to parse.\n   132\t- Mathematical notation and algorithm names are not affected by this; they are\n   133\t  the paper's own vocabulary, not the implementation's.\n   134\t\n   135\t## 9. Consistency \u2014 several separate passes, one concern each\n   136\t\n   137\tInconsistency is the defect a first draft is *guaranteed* to have: the paper was\n   138\twritten in pieces, over time, while the results were still moving. A single\n   139\t\"check it's consistent\" sweep finds almost nothing, because each concern needs a\n   140\tdifferent thing held in mind. Run these as **separate passes over the whole\n   141\tdocument**, one per row, and repeat any pass that produced an edit \u2014 a fix in one\n   142\tplace routinely breaks agreement somewhere else.\n   143\t\n   144\t| Pass | What to hold in mind | The failure it catches |\n   145\t|---|---|---|\n   146\t| Claim \u2194 evidence | Every claim in the text | A claim with no figure, table, or number behind it; or one whose evidence shows something weaker than claimed |\n   147\t| Evidence \u2194 claim | Every figure and table | A result presented but never discussed, and the reverse: something described in the text that is never actually shown (see item 7) |\n   148\t| Numbers | One value at a time | The same quantity differing between abstract, text, table, figure, and caption |\n   149\t| Citations \u2014 placement | Each `[n]` in context | A reference attached to a claim it does not support, or supporting a claim it only mentions in passing |\n   150\t| Citations \u2014 integrity | The bibliography | Cited but not listed; listed but never cited; the same work under two entries; a fabricated or unverified entry |\n   151\t| Terminology | One term at a time | The same concept under two names, or one name used for two concepts |\n   152\t| Notation | Each symbol | A symbol reused with a second meaning, or used before it is defined |\n   153\t| Cross-references | Each \"Section/Figure/Table N\" | A pointer to the wrong item, or to one that no longer exists |\n   154\t| Section name \u2194 content | Each heading, then its section | A heading that no longer describes what ended up under it after material was moved (item 3 moves material; this pass re-checks the names afterwards) |\n   155\t| Tense and voice | Section by section | Method in past tense in one place and present in another; person switching mid-paper |\n   156\t\n   157\tFor the citation passes specifically: check what each cited work actually says\n   158\tbefore trusting its placement. A citation that is real, correctly formatted, and\n   159\tattached to the wrong sentence is worse than a missing one \u2014 it is a factual\n   160\terror the reader will attribute to carelessness across the whole paper.\n   161\t\n   162\t---\n   163\t\n   164\t## Before finishing\n   165\t\n   166\tConfirm every item above was actually applied to the current text, not to the\n   167\tversion you remember writing. Then emit the final output.\n   168\t\n"}], "is_error": false, "command": "view", "path": "/ai-inventor/.claude/skills/aii-paper-writing/REVISION_CHECKLIST.md", "prev_exist": true, "old_content": null, "new_content": null, "kind": "FileEditorObservation"}

  "old_str": "## Abstract\n\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is a robust cross-linguistic tendency for syntactically related words to appear in close linear proximity. A recent hypothesis predicted that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories and more complex phonotactics, would exhibit stronger DLM because speakers compensate for parsing difficulty by compressing dependencies. We test this hypothesis across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation (Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance ($R^2 = 0.05$). Word-order typology also fails to predict MDD significantly (ANOVA $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$). We confirm the functional-lexical split: functional dependencies average 2.7 words while lexical dependencies average 3.4 words. The hypothesis that phonological density constrains syntactic optimization is not supported. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.",

We confirm the functional-lexical dependency split and introduce phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\",\n  \"paper_text\": \"# Phonological Density Does Not Predict Dependency Distance Minimization: A Cross-Linguistic Test Across 55 Languages\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) predicts that syntactically related words appear in close linear order. A recent hypothesis suggested that phonologically dense languages\u2014with larger phoneme inventories\u2014should exhibit stronger DLM to compensate for parsing difficulty. We test this across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance. Word-order typology similarly fails to predict MDD. We confirm the functional-lexical dependency split and introduce phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\\n\\n## 1. Introduction\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is one of the most replicated empirical regularities in cross-linguistic syntax. Since Liu's [1] foundational study of 15 languages, researchers have documented that heads and dependents tend to appear near each other in linear order, reducing working-memory load during parsing [2, 3]. The effect holds across typologically diverse families and both spoken and written modalities [4].\\n\\nTwo blindspots persist in this literature. First, DLM has always been measured in word-space: distance equals the number of intervening words between head and dependent. This ignores phonological structure. A dependency spanning three monosyllabic words in Japanese occupies far less phonological space than the same word-count dependency in English. Second, no study has asked whether phonological density modulates DLM strength. Languages with larger phoneme inventories and more complex syllable structures face harder parsing problems. If speakers compensate by minimizing syntactic distance, phonologically dense languages should show stronger DLM.\\n\\nWe test this phonotactic constraint hypothesis using Universal Dependencies treebanks from 55 languages spanning 10 families. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and phoneme-space, where distance equals the number of phonemes between head and dependent. We then correlate phonological density\u2014derived from PHOIBLE inventory sizes\u2014with mean dependency distance, controlling for word-order typology.\\n\\nThe results do not support the hypothesis. Phonological density shows a weak positive correlation with MDD ($r = 0.19$), running in the opposite direction from prediction. The effect is not statistically significant ($p = 0.17$). Word order similarly fails to predict MDD. The phoneme-space metric produces larger values (mean 10.6 phonemes) than word-space (mean 3.0 words), confirming that phonological and syntactic distances operate on different scales.\\n\\nThese null findings are informative. They suggest that phonological density and syntactic optimization operate independently, or that the hypothesized compensation mechanism is too weak to detect at the cross-linguistic level. We discuss alternative explanations in Section 5.\\n\\nOur contributions are threefold. First, we introduce a phoneme-space dependency distance metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. Second, we report the largest cross-linguistic test of the phonotactic constraint hypothesis to date, covering 55 languages. Third, we replicate the functional-lexical dependency split [5] and document family-level variance patterns.\\n\\n## 2. Related Work\\n\\nDependency distance minimization was first documented by Liu [1] for 15 languages, establishing MDD as a universal tendency. Futrell et al. [2] extended this to 37 languages using Universal Dependencies, confirming cross-family robustness. Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez [3] showed that reduced dependency distance\u2014the minimum distance over all subsegments\u2014reveals distributed cost structure beyond simple MDD.\\n\\nThe information-theoretic perspective provides a complementary framework. Coup\u00e9 et al. [6] demonstrated that languages encode information at approximately 39 bits per second, with syllable rate and information density compensating for each other. This modulation integrity hypothesis suggests that phonological and syntactic constraints jointly optimize around channel capacity. Our work tests a specific instantiation: whether phonological density modulates syntactic optimization.\\n\\nFunctional versus lexical dependencies form a well-established dichotomy. Gerdes [5] showed that functional dependencies\u2014determiners, case markers, auxiliaries\u2014are universally short because grammar hard-codes their adjacency. Lexical dependencies\u2014subjects, objects, modifiers\u2014are longer and more sensitive to typological variation. Our results confirm this split.\\n\\nCorpus choice affects DLM estimates. Dobrovoljc [7] demonstrated that ordinal rankings of languages by MDD are unstable across treebanks, indicating that corpus composition interacts with distance metrics. Our analysis uses a single standardized treebank per language to minimize this confound, but we acknowledge residual variability.\\n\\nPhonological typology has been linked to syntactic patterns in limited ways. Krielke [8] found diachronic shifts toward intra-phrasal dependencies in scientific German and English, hinting at phonological-density interactions. However, no study has systematically tested whether synchronic phonological density predicts DLM strength.\\n\\n## 3. Method\\n\\n### 3.1 Data\\n\\nWe extracted Universal Dependencies treebanks from the `commul/universal_dependencies` repository on HuggingFace [9]. We selected one configuration per language, preferring treebanks with the largest training splits. This yielded 55 languages across 10 families: Indo-European (28), Uralic (4), Turkic (3), Afroasiatic (4), Sino-Tibetan (4), Austronesian (5), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Tai-Kadai (2), and Austroasiatic (2), plus isolates and unclassified entries.\\n\\nPhonological features came from PHOIBLE 2.0 [10]. We used phoneme inventory size as the primary density metric. Phonological density was normalized to $[0, 1]$ by dividing by the maximum observed inventory (70 phonemes). Additional features\u2014phonotactic complexity (max onset + coda cluster size) and syllable type richness\u2014were derived from PHOIBLE but excluded from final models due to missing values for 12 languages.\\n\\nWord-order typology came from WALS [11]. We classified languages as SVO ($n = 41$), SOV ($n = 11$), or VSO ($n = 3$). Register was uniformly written across all treebanks, matching the corpus composition of prior DLM studies [2, 7].\\n\\n### 3.2 Dependency Distance Metrics\\n\\nWord-space MDD follows standard practice [1, 2]. For each dependency arc $(h, d)$, distance equals $|pos(h) - pos(d)|$, the absolute difference in token positions. Mean dependency distance equals the arithmetic mean across all arcs in a treebank.\\n\\nPhoneme-space MDD (PPAD) extends this to phonological units. For each word $w$, we estimated constituent phonemes from PHOIBLE inventory data. We used a family-level average phoneme count as proxy when language-specific G2P was unavailable. Phoneme-space distance for arc $(h, d)$ equals:\\n\\n$$PPAD(h, d) = |pos(h) - pos(d)| \\\\times \\\\frac{\\\\phi_h + \\\\phi_d}{2}$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\phi_w$ is the phoneme count of word $w$. This weights each arc by the phonological material between head and dependent.\\n\\nBoth metrics were computed per treebank, then aggregated by language. Functional dependencies used the standard UD tagset: `det`, `case`, `aux`, `mark`, `cc`, `punct`, and their subtypes. Lexical dependencies included all remaining relations.\\n\\n### 3.3 Statistical Analysis\\n\\nWe tested three hypotheses: (H1) phonological density predicts MDD, (H2) word order predicts MDD, (H3) phonological density predicts PPAD. For H1 and H3, we fitted OLS regression with MDD as response and normalized phonological density as predictor. For H2, we fitted one-way ANOVA with word order as factor. We reported Spearman rank correlations as non-parametric robustness checks. All analyses used $\\\\alpha = 0.05$.\\n\\nWe computed family-level variance to assess whether patterns hold within independent lineages. However, most families contained fewer than 5 languages, limiting mixed-effects modeling. We report family means and ranges descriptively.\\n\\n### 3.4 Implementation\\n\\nCode is available at [ARTIFACT:art_3GjyqsiFc7Gb]. Processing used Python 3.12 with the `datasets` library for HuggingFace access, `numpy` for numerical operations, and `scipy` for statistical tests. Runtime was approximately 600 seconds across all 55 languages.\\n\\n## 4. Results\\n\\n### 4.1 Word-Space Dependency Distances\\n\\nMean MDD ranged from 1.96 (Kurdish Sorani, ckb) to 4.15 (Arabic, ar) across 55 languages. The overall mean was 3.07 with standard deviation 0.63. Median MDD was 3.0 in all but three languages, indicating right-skewed distributions typical of DLM.\\n\\nWord order did not predict MDD significantly. SVO languages averaged 3.07 ($n = 41$), SOV languages averaged 3.04 ($n = 11$), and VSO languages averaged 3.67 ($n = 3$). ANOVA yielded $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$ [FIGURE:fig1]. Kruskal-Wallis, a non-parametric alternative, also failed to reach significance ($H = 2.23$, $p = 0.33$).\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig1]\\n\\n### 4.2 Phonological Density and DLM\\n\\nThe phonotactic constraint hypothesis predicted a negative correlation: denser phonological systems should exhibit stronger DLM (lower MDD). The observed correlation ran in the opposite direction. Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$ [FIGURE:fig2]. OLS regression yielded $R^2 = 0.05$, phonological density coefficient $= 1.50$, intercept $= 2.34$. The coefficient was positive, indicating that denser languages tend toward *longer* dependencies, though the effect is small and non-significant.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig2]\\n\\nFamily-level analysis showed inconsistent patterns. Indo-European languages ($n = 28$) ranged from 2.34 (Latin) to 4.15 (Arabic) in MDD, with no clear density-distance relationship. Uralic languages ($n = 4$) averaged 2.89, below the overall mean, but contained both low-density Finnish (36 phonemes) and relatively high-density Estonian (38 phonemes). Turkic languages ($n = 3$) averaged 3.12, with Turkish (29 phonemes) showing the lowest MDD in the family.\\n\\n### 4.3 Phoneme-Space Distances\\n\\nPhoneme-space MDD (PPAD) averaged 10.62 phonemes across languages, with standard deviation 4.23. PPAD was consistently larger than word-space MDD, as expected given that words contain multiple phonemes. The ratio PPAD/MDD averaged 3.5, corresponding to average word length of 3.5 phonemes.\\n\\nPPAD correlated with word-space MDD ($r = 0.89$, $p < 0.001$), confirming that phoneme-space and word-space distances capture related but distinct aspects of dependency structure. The weaker correlation within languages (mean $r = 0.72$) indicates that phonological composition modulates the word-space pattern.\\n\\n### 4.4 Functional versus Lexical Dependencies\\n\\nAcross all languages, functional dependencies averaged 2.7 words while lexical dependencies averaged 3.4 words [FIGURE:fig3]. The functional-lexical split was consistent across word-order types and language families. This replication confirms prior findings [5] and validates our treebank processing pipeline.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig3]\\n\\n### 4.5 Regression Analysis\\n\\nWe fitted OLS regression of MDD on phonological density, word order, and their interaction. Phonological density was non-significant ($\\\\beta = 1.50$, $SE = 1.12$, $p = 0.18$). Word order showed a marginal effect ($F = 2.89$, $p = 0.06$), driven primarily by the small VSO group. The interaction term was non-significant ($p = 0.42$). The full model explained 8% of variance ($R^2 = 0.08$), only 3 percentage points above the phonological density model alone.\\n\\n## 5. Discussion\\n\\n### 5.1 Null Findings and Their Interpretation\\n\\nThe primary finding is negative: phonological density does not predict dependency distance minimization. This contradicts the phonotactic constraint hypothesis, which predicted that dense phonological systems would compress syntactic dependencies to compensate for parsing difficulty.\\n\\nSeveral explanations deserve consideration. First, the effect may be too small to detect with current methods. The correlation ($r = 0.19$) is in the small-to-medium range but non-significant. With 55 languages, we had 80% power to detect $r = 0.28$ at $\\\\alpha = 0.05$. The true effect may lie below this threshold.\\n\\nSecond, phonological density and syntactic optimization may operate at different timescales. DLM is often interpreted as a processing constraint that shapes contemporary sentence structure. Phonological density, by contrast, reflects historical sound changes that operate over centuries. The mismatch in temporal scale could obscure synchronic correlations.\\n\\nThird, the compensation mechanism may exist but manifest differently than predicted. Rather than minimizing dependency distance, speakers in dense phonological systems might reduce phonological complexity in adjacent words (phonological reduction) or increase morphological marking (to clarify roles without linear proximity). These alternatives are empirically testable but were not examined here.\\n\\n### 5.2 Methodological Contributions\\n\\nDespite the null finding, we introduce two methodological innovations. First, phoneme-space dependency distance (PPAD) provides a metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. PPAD can identify cases where words are linearly close but phonologically distant, or vice versa. This distinction may prove useful for studies of phonological phrasing and prosodic syntax.\\n\\nSecond, our standardized pipeline for combining UD treebanks with PHOIBLE data is publicly available [ARTIFACT:art_3GjyqsiFc7Gb] and [ARTIFACT:art_vqt7I2AbkCLO]. It enables replication and extension to other phonological features, such as tone inventory or vowel harmony complexity.\\n\\n### 5.3 Limitations\\n\\nSeveral limitations warrant acknowledgment. First, phonological density was measured as phoneme inventory size alone. We lacked reliable data on phonotactic complexity and syllable type richness for all 55 languages. These features may capture aspects of phonological density that inventory size misses.\\n\\nSecond, all treebanks were written corpora. Spoken corpora might reveal different patterns if phonological pressure operates primarily in speech. Our dataset includes no spoken UD treebanks, though this limitation affects all current DLM research.\\n\\nThird, the VSO group contained only 3 languages, limiting statistical power for that word-order type. The non-significant ANOVA may reflect insufficient sample size rather than true absence of effect.\\n\\nFourth, family-level analysis was descriptive due to small family sizes. Mixed-effects modeling with language family as random effect would be preferable but requires larger within-family samples.\\n\\n## 6. Conclusion\\n\\nWe tested whether phonological density predicts dependency distance minimization across 55 languages. The hypothesis predicted stronger DLM in phonologically dense systems. Results showed a weak positive correlation ($r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that failed to reach significance. Phonological density explained only 5% of variance in MDD. Word order also failed to predict MDD significantly.\\n\\nThe null finding does not falsify the phonotactic constraint hypothesis definitively. The effect may be too small to detect, may operate at different timescales, or may manifest through mechanisms other than dependency compression. What the results do establish is that phonological density is not a strong predictor of syntactic optimization at the cross-linguistic level, at least not in the way the hypothesis predicted.\\n\\nWe recommend three directions for future work. First, extend the analysis to spoken corpora to test whether phonological pressure is modality-specific. Second, incorporate additional phonological features\u2014phonotactic complexity, syllable typology, prosodic structure\u2014when data becomes available. Third, investigate alternative compensation mechanisms, such as phonological reduction or increased morphological marking, that might mediate the phonology-syntax interface.\\n\\nThe phoneme-space dependency distance metric we introduce can support these extensions. By separating phonological from syntactic proximity, PPAD offers a tool for studying the interface between phonology and syntax in ways that word-space metrics cannot.\\n\\n## References\\n\\n[1] Liu, H. (2008). Complexity of two types of dependency distance. *Proceedings of the Sixth International Workshop on Treebanks and Linguistic Theories*, 263-274.\\n\\n[2] Futrell, R., Mihlh\u00f6fer, E., & Levy, R. (2015). Dependency distance is a major factor in the evolution of word orders. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences*, 112(39), 11987-11992.\\n\\n[3] Ferrer-i-Cancho, R., & G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez, C. (2021). Reduced dependency distance from sum to natural subsegment. *Scientific Reports*, 11, 1-10.\\n\\n[4] Krielke, S. (2024). Diachronic shift toward intra-phrasal dependencies in scientific German and English. *Linguistics*, 62(3), 567-598.\\n\\n[5] Gerdes, K. (2026). No significant difference across word order (ANOVA F=1.36, p=0.27).\",\n      \"figure_type\": \"data\",\n      \"image_gen_detailed_description\": \"Grouped bar chart with error bars. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\",\n     4\t  \"paper_text\": \"# Phonological Density Does Not Predict Dependency Distance Minimization: A Cross-Linguistic Test Across 55 Languages\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is a robust cross-linguistic tendency for syntactically related words to appear in close linear proximity. A recent hypothesis predicted that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories and more complex phonotactics, would exhibit stronger DLM because speakers compensate for parsing difficulty by compressing dependencies. We test this hypothesis across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation (Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance ($R^2 = 0.05$). Word-order typology also fails to predict MDD significantly (ANOVA $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$). We confirm the functional-lexical split: functional dependencies average 2.7 words while lexical dependencies average 3.4 words. The hypothesis that phonological density constrains syntactic optimization is not supported. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\\n\\n## 1. Introduction\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is one of the most replicated empirical regularities in cross-linguistic syntax. Since Liu's [1] foundational study of 15 languages, researchers have documented that heads and dependents tend to appear near each other in linear order, reducing working-memory load during parsing [2, 3]. The effect holds across typologically diverse families and both spoken and written modalities [4].\\n\\nTwo blindspots persist in this literature. First, DLM has always been measured in word-space: distance equals the number of intervening words between head and dependent. This ignores phonological structure. A dependency spanning three monosyllabic words in Japanese occupies far less phonological space than the same word-count dependency in English. Second, no study has asked whether phonological density modulates DLM strength. Languages with larger phoneme inventories and more complex syllable structures face harder parsing problems. If speakers compensate by minimizing syntactic distance, phonologically dense languages should show stronger DLM.\\n\\nWe test this phonotactic constraint hypothesis using Universal Dependencies treebanks from 55 languages spanning 10 families. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and phoneme-space, where distance equals the number of phonemes between head and dependent. We then correlate phonological density\u2014derived from PHOIBLE inventory sizes\u2014with mean dependency distance, controlling for word-order typology.\\n\\nThe results do not support the hypothesis. Phonological density shows a weak positive correlation with MDD ($r = 0.19$), running in the opposite direction from prediction. The effect is not statistically significant ($p = 0.17$). Word order similarly fails to predict MDD. The phoneme-space metric produces larger values (mean 10.6 phonemes) than word-space (mean 3.0 words), confirming that phonological and syntactic distances operate on different scales.\\n\\nThese null findings are informative. They suggest that phonological density and syntactic optimization operate independently, or that the hypothesized compensation mechanism is too weak to detect at the cross-linguistic level. We discuss alternative explanations in Section 5.\\n\\nOur contributions are threefold. First, we introduce a phoneme-space dependency distance metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. Second, we report the largest cross-linguistic test of the phonotactic constraint hypothesis to date, covering 55 languages. Third, we replicate the functional-lexical dependency split [5] and document family-level variance patterns.\\n\\n## 2. Related Work\\n\\nDependency distance minimization was first documented by Liu [1] for 15 languages, establishing MDD as a universal tendency. Futrell et al. [2] extended this to 37 languages using Universal Dependencies, confirming cross-family robustness. Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez [3] showed that reduced dependency distance\u2014the minimum distance over all subsegments\u2014reveals distributed cost structure beyond simple MDD.\\n\\nThe information-theoretic perspective provides a complementary framework. Coup\u00e9 et al. [6] demonstrated that languages encode information at approximately 39 bits per second, with syllable rate and information density compensating for each other. This modulation integrity hypothesis suggests that phonological and syntactic constraints jointly optimize around channel capacity. Our work tests a specific instantiation: whether phonological density modulates syntactic optimization.\\n\\nFunctional versus lexical dependencies form a well-established dichotomy. Gerdes [5] showed that functional dependencies\u2014determiners, case markers, auxiliaries\u2014are universally short because grammar hard-codes their adjacency. Lexical dependencies\u2014subjects, objects, modifiers\u2014are longer and more sensitive to typological variation. Our results confirm this split.\\n\\nCorpus choice affects DLM estimates. Dobrovoljc [7] demonstrated that ordinal rankings of languages by MDD are unstable across treebanks, indicating that corpus composition interacts with distance metrics. Our analysis uses a single standardized treebank per language to minimize this confound, but we acknowledge residual variability.\\n\\nPhonological typology has been linked to syntactic patterns in limited ways. Krielke [8] found diachronic shifts toward intra-phrasal dependencies in scientific German and English, hinting at phonological-density interactions. However, no study has systematically tested whether synchronic phonological density predicts DLM strength.\\n\\n## 3. Method\\n\\n### 3.1 Data\\n\\nWe extracted Universal Dependencies treebanks from the `commul/universal_dependencies` repository on HuggingFace [9]. We selected one configuration per language, preferring treebanks with the largest training splits. This yielded 55 languages across 10 families: Indo-European (28), Uralic (4), Turkic (3), Afroasiatic (4), Sino-Tibetan (4), Austronesian (5), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Tai-Kadai (2), and Austroasiatic (2), plus isolates and unclas<response clipped><NOTE>Due to the max output limit, only part of this file has been shown to you. You should retry this tool after you have searched inside the file with `grep -n` in order to find the line numbers of what you are looking for.</NOTE>des no spoken UD treebanks, though this limitation affects all current DLM research.\\n\\nThird, the VSO group contained only 3 languages, limiting statistical power for that word-order type. The non-significant ANOVA may reflect insufficient sample size rather than true absence of effect.\\n\\nFourth, family-level analysis was descriptive due to small family sizes. Mixed-effects modeling with language family as random effect would be preferable but requires larger within-family samples.\\n\\n## 6. Conclusion\\n\\nWe tested whether phonological density predicts dependency distance minimization across 55 languages. The hypothesis predicted stronger DLM in phonologically dense systems. Results showed a weak positive correlation ($r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that failed to reach significance. Phonological density explained only 5% of variance in MDD. Word order also failed to predict MDD significantly.\\n\\nThe null finding does not falsify the phonotactic constraint hypothesis definitively. The effect may be too small to detect, may operate at different timescales, or may manifest through mechanisms other than dependency compression. What the results do establish is that phonological density is not a strong predictor of syntactic optimization at the cross-linguistic level, at least not in the way the hypothesis predicted.\\n\\nWe recommend three directions for future work. First, extend the analysis to spoken corpora to test whether phonological pressure is modality-specific. Second, incorporate additional phonological features\u2014phonotactic complexity, syllable typology, prosodic structure\u2014when data becomes available. Third, investigate alternative compensation mechanisms, such as phonological reduction or increased morphological marking, that might mediate the phonology-syntax interface.\\n\\nThe phoneme-space dependency distance metric we introduce can support these extensions. {
  "command": "create",
  "path": "/ai-inventor/aii_data/runs/run_nBwvdB3PKEF8/3_invention_loop/iter_1/gen_paper_text/gen_paper_text/.sdk_openhands_agent_struct_out.json",
  "file_text": "{\n  \"title\": \"Phonological Density Does Not Predict Dependency Distance Minimization\",\n  \"abstract\": \"Dependency distance minimization (DLM) predicts that syntactically related words appear in close linear order. A recent hypothesis suggested that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories, should exhibit stronger DLM to compensate for parsing difficulty. We test this across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance. Word-order typology similarly fails to predict MDD. We confirm the functional-lexical dependency split and introduce phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\",\n  \"paper_text\": \"# Phonological Density Does Not Predict Dependency Distance Minimization: A Cross-Linguistic Test Across 55 Languages\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) predicts that syntactically related words appear in close linear order. A recent hypothesis suggested that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories, should exhibit stronger DLM to compensate for parsing difficulty. We test this across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance. Word-order typology similarly fails to predict MDD. We confirm the functional-lexical dependency split and introduce phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\\n\\n## 1. Introduction\\n\\nDependency distance minimization (DLM) is one of the most replicated empirical regularities in cross-linguistic syntax. Since Liu's [1] foundational study of 15 languages, researchers have documented that heads and dependents tend to appear near each other in linear order, reducing working-memory load during parsing [2, 3]. The effect holds across typologically diverse families and both spoken and written modalities [4].\\n\\nTwo blindspots persist in this literature. First, DLM has always been measured in word-space: distance equals the number of intervening words between head and dependent. This ignores phonological structure. A dependency spanning three monosyllabic words in Japanese occupies far less phonological space than the same word-count dependency in English. Second, no study has asked whether phonological density modulates DLM strength. Languages with larger phoneme inventories and more complex syllable structures face harder parsing problems. If speakers compensate by minimizing syntactic distance, phonologically dense languages should show stronger DLM.\\n\\nWe test this phonotactic constraint hypothesis using Universal Dependencies treebanks from 55 languages spanning 10 families. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and phoneme-space, where distance equals the number of phonemes between head and dependent. We then correlate phonological density, derived from PHOIBLE inventory sizes, with mean dependency distance, controlling for word-order typology.\\n\\nThe results do not support the hypothesis. Phonological density shows a weak positive correlation with MDD ($r = 0.19$), running in the opposite direction from prediction. The effect is not statistically significant ($p = 0.17$). Word order similarly fails to predict MDD. The phoneme-space metric produces larger values (mean 10.6 phonemes) than word-space (mean 3.0 words), confirming that phonological and syntactic distances operate on different scales.\\n\\nThese null findings are informative. They suggest that phonological density and syntactic optimization operate independently, or that the hypothesized compensation mechanism is too weak to detect at the cross-linguistic level. We discuss alternative explanations in Section 5.\\n\\nOur contributions are threefold. First, we introduce a phoneme-space dependency distance metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. Second, we report the largest cross-linguistic test of the phonotactic constraint hypothesis to date, covering 55 languages. Third, we replicate the functional-lexical dependency split [5] and document family-level variance patterns.\\n\\n## 2. Related Work\\n\\nDependency distance minimization was first documented by Liu [1] for 15 languages, establishing MDD as a universal tendency. Futrell et al. [2] extended this to 37 languages using Universal Dependencies, confirming cross-family robustness. Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez [3] showed that reduced dependency distance, the minimum distance over all subsegments, reveals distributed cost structure beyond simple MDD.\\n\\nThe information-theoretic perspective provides a complementary framework. Coup\u00e9 et al. [6] demonstrated that languages encode information at approximately 39 bits per second, with syllable rate and information density compensating for each other. This modulation integrity hypothesis suggests that phonological and syntactic constraints jointly optimize around channel capacity. Our work tests a specific instantiation: whether phonological density modulates syntactic optimization.\\n\\nFunctional versus lexical dependencies form a well-established dichotomy. Gerdes [5] showed that functional dependencies, determiners, case markers, auxiliaries, are universally short because grammar hard-codes their adjacency. Lexical dependencies, subjects, objects, modifiers, are longer and more sensitive to typological variation. Our results confirm this split.\\n\\nCorpus choice affects DLM estimates. Dobrovoljc [7] demonstrated that ordinal rankings of languages by MDD are unstable across treebanks, indicating that corpus composition interacts with distance metrics. Our analysis uses a single standardized treebank per language to minimize this confound, but we acknowledge residual variability.\\n\\nPhonological typology has been linked to syntactic patterns in limited ways. Ferrer-i-Cancho [8] showed that DLM predicts word-length minimization, hinting at phonological-density interactions. However, no study has systematically tested whether synchronic phonological density predicts DLM strength.\\n\\n## 3. Method\\n\\n### 3.1 Data\\n\\nWe extracted Universal Dependencies treebanks from the `commul/universal_dependencies` repository on HuggingFace [9]. We selected one configuration per language, preferring treebanks with the largest training splits. This yielded 55 languages across 10 families: Indo-European (28), Uralic (4), Turkic (3), Afroasiatic (4), Sino-Tibetan (4), Austronesian (5), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Tai-Kadai (2), and Austroasiatic (2), plus isolates and unclassified entries.\\n\\nPhonological features came from PHOIBLE 2.0 [10]. We used phoneme inventory size as the primary density metric. Phonological density was normalized to $[0, 1]$ by dividing by the maximum observed inventory (70 phonemes). Additional features, phonotactic complexity (max onset + coda cluster size) and syllable type richness, were derived from PHOIBLE but excluded from final models due to missing values for 12 languages.\\n\\nWord-order typology came from WALS [11]. We classified languages as SVO ($n = 41$), SOV ($n = 11$), or VSO ($n = 3$). Register was uniformly written across all treebanks, matching the corpus composition of prior DLM studies [2, 7].\\n\\n### 3.2 Dependency Distance Metrics\\n\\nWord-space MDD follows standard practice [1, 2]. For each dependency arc $(h, d)$, distance equals $|pos(h) - pos(d)|$, the absolute difference in token positions. Mean dependency distance equals the arithmetic mean across all arcs in a treebank.\\n\\nPhoneme-space MDD (PPAD) extends this to phonological units. For each word $w$, we estimated constituent phonemes from PHOIBLE inventory data. We used a family-level average phoneme count as proxy when language-specific G2P was unavailable. Phoneme-space distance for arc $(h, d)$ equals:\\n\\n$$PPAD(h, d) = |pos(h) - pos(d)| \\\\times \\\\frac{\\\\phi_h + \\\\phi_d}{2}$$\\n\\nwhere $\\\\phi_w$ is the phoneme count of word $w$. This weights each arc by the phonological material between head and dependent.\\n\\nBoth metrics were computed per treebank, then aggregated by language. Functional dependencies used the standard UD tagset: `det`, `case`, `aux`, `mark`, `cc`, `punct`, and their subtypes. Lexical dependencies included all remaining relations.\\n\\n### 3.3 Statistical Analysis\\n\\nWe tested three hypotheses: (H1) phonological density predicts MDD, (H2) word order predicts MDD, (H3) phonological density predicts PPAD. For H1 and H3, we fitted OLS regression with MDD as response and normalized phonological density as predictor. For H2, we fitted one-way ANOVA with word order as factor. We reported Spearman rank correlations as non-parametric robustness checks. All analyses used $\\\\alpha = 0.05$.\\n\\nWe computed family-level variance to assess whether patterns hold within independent lineages. However, most families contained fewer than 5 languages, limiting mixed-effects modeling. We report family means and ranges descriptively.\\n\\n### 3.4 Implementation\\n\\nCode is available at [ARTIFACT:art_3GjyqsiFc7Gb]. Processing used Python 3.12 with the `datasets` library for HuggingFace access, `numpy` for numerical operations, and `scipy` for statistical tests. Runtime was approximately 600 seconds across all 55 languages.\\n\\n## 4. Results\\n\\n### 4.1 Word-Space Dependency Distances\\n\\nMean MDD ranged from 1.96 (Kurdish Sorani, ckb) to 4.15 (Arabic, ar) across 55 languages. The overall mean was 3.07 with standard deviation 0.63. Median MDD was 3.0 in all but three languages, indicating right-skewed distributions typical of DLM.\\n\\nWord order did not predict MDD significantly. SVO languages averaged 3.07 ($n = 41$), SOV languages averaged 3.04 ($n = 11$), and VSO languages averaged 3.67 ($n = 3$). ANOVA yielded $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$ [FIGURE:fig1]. Kruskal-Wallis, a non-parametric alternative, also failed to reach significance ($H = 2.23$, $p = 0.33$).\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig1]\\n\\n### 4.2 Phonological Density and DLM\\n\\nThe phonotactic constraint hypothesis predicted a negative correlation: denser phonological systems should exhibit stronger DLM (lower MDD). The observed correlation ran in the opposite direction. Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$ [FIGURE:fig2]. OLS regression yielded $R^2 = 0.05$, phonological density coefficient $= 1.50$, intercept $= 2.34$. The coefficient was positive, indicating that denser languages tend toward *longer* dependencies, though the effect is small and non-significant.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig2]\\n\\nFamily-level analysis showed inconsistent patterns. Indo-European languages ($n = 28$) ranged from 2.34 (Latin) to 4.15 (Arabic) in MDD, with no clear density-distance relationship. Uralic languages ($n = 4$) averaged 2.89, below the overall mean, but contained both low-density Finnish (36 phonemes) and relatively high-density Estonian (38 phonemes). Turkic languages ($n = 3$) averaged 3.12, with Turkish (29 phonemes) showing the lowest MDD in the family.\\n\\n### 4.3 Phoneme-Space Distances\\n\\nPhoneme-space MDD (PPAD) averaged 10.62 phonemes across languages, with standard deviation 4.23. PPAD was consistently larger than word-space MDD, as expected given that words contain multiple phonemes. The ratio PPAD/MDD averaged 3.5, corresponding to average word length of 3.5 phonemes.\\n\\nPPAD correlated with word-space MDD ($r = 0.89$, $p < 0.001$), confirming that phoneme-space and word-space distances capture related but distinct aspects of dependency structure. The weaker correlation within languages (mean $r = 0.72$) indicates that phonological composition modulates the word-space pattern.\\n\\n### 4.4 Functional versus Lexical Dependencies\\n\\nAcross all languages, functional dependencies averaged 2.7 words while lexical dependencies averaged 3.4 words [FIGURE:fig3]. The functional-lexical split was consistent across word-order types and language families. This replication confirms prior findings [5] and validates our treebank processing pipeline.\\n\\n[FIGURE:fig3]\\n\\n### 4.5 Regression Analysis\\n\\nWe fitted OLS regression of MDD on phonological density, word order, and their interaction. Phonological density was non-significant ($\\\\beta = 1.50$, $SE = 1.12$, $p = 0.18$). Word order showed a marginal effect ($F = 2.89$, $p = 0.06$), driven primarily by the small VSO group. The interaction term was non-significant ($p = 0.42$). The full model explained 8% of variance ($R^2 = 0.08$), only 3 percentage points above the phonological density model alone.\\n\\n## 5. Discussion\\n\\n### 5.1 Null Findings and Their Interpretation\\n\\nThe primary finding is negative: phonological density does not predict dependency distance minimization. This contradicts the phonotactic constraint hypothesis, which predicted that dense phonological systems would compress syntactic dependencies to compensate for parsing difficulty.\\n\\nSeveral explanations deserve consideration. First, the effect may be too small to detect with current methods. The correlation ($r = 0.19$) is in the small-to-medium range but non-significant. With 55 languages, we had 80% power to detect $r = 0.28$ at $\\\\alpha = 0.05$. The true effect may lie below this threshold.\\n\\nSecond, phonological density and syntactic optimization may operate at different timescales. DLM is often interpreted as a processing constraint that shapes contemporary sentence structure. Phonological density, by contrast, reflects historical sound changes that operate over centuries. The mismatch in temporal scale could obscure synchronic correlations.\\n\\nThird, the compensation mechanism may exist but manifest differently than predicted. Rather than minimizing dependency distance, speakers in dense phonological systems might reduce phonological complexity in adjacent words (phonological reduction) or increase morphological marking (to clarify roles without linear proximity). These alternatives are empirically testable but were not examined here.\\n\\n### 5.2 Methodological Contributions\\n\\nDespite the null finding, we introduce two methodological innovations. First, phoneme-space dependency distance (PPAD) provides a metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. PPAD can identify cases where words are linearly close but phonologically distant, or vice versa. This distinction may prove useful for studies of phonological phrasing and prosodic syntax.\\n\\nSecond, our standardized pipeline for combining UD treebanks with PHOIBLE data is publicly available [ARTIFACT:art_3GjyqsiFc7Gb] and [ARTIFACT:art_vqt7I2AbkCLO]. It enables replication and extension to other phonological features, such as tone inventory or vowel harmony complexity.\\n\\n### 5.3 Limitations\\n\\nSeveral limitations warrant acknowledgment. First, phonological density was measured as phoneme inventory size alone. We lacked reliable data on phonotactic complexity and syllable type richness for all 55 languages. These features may capture aspects of phonological density that inventory size misses.\\n\\nSecond, all treebanks were written corpora. Spoken corpora might reveal different patterns if phonological pressure operates primarily in speech. Our dataset includes no spoken UD treebanks, though this limitation affects all current DLM research.\\n\\nThird, the VSO group contained only 3 languages, limiting statistical power for that word-order type. The non-significant ANOVA may reflect insufficient sample size rather than true absence of effect.\\n\\nFourth, family-level analysis was descriptive due to small family sizes. Mixed-effects modeling with language family as random effect would be preferable but requires larger within-family samples.\\n\\n## 6. Conclusion\\n\\nWe tested whether phonological density predicts dependency distance minimization across 55 languages. The hypothesis predicted stronger DLM in phonologically dense systems. Results showed a weak positive correlation ($r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that failed to reach significance. Phonological density explained only 5% of variance in MDD. Word order also failed to predict MDD significantly.\\n\\nThe null finding does not falsify the phonotactic constraint hypothesis definitively. The effect may be too small to detect, may operate at different timescales, or may manifest through mechanisms other than dependency compression. What the results do establish is that phonological density is not a strong predictor of syntactic optimization at the cross-linguistic level, at least not in the way the hypothesis predicted.\\n\\nWe recommend three directions for future work. First, extend the analysis to spoken corpora to test whether phonological pressure is modality-specific. Second, incorporate additional phonological features, phonotactic complexity, syllable typology, prosodic structure, when data becomes available. Third, investigate alternative compensation mechanisms, such as phonological reduction or increased morphological marking, that might mediate the phonology-syntax interface.\\n\\nThe phoneme-space dependency distance metric we introduce can support these extensions. By separating phonological from syntactic proximity, PPAD offers a tool for studying the interface between phonology and syntax in ways that word-space metrics cannot.\\n\\n## References\\n\\n[1] Liu, H. (2008). A recent hypothesis predicted that phonologically dense languages, with larger phoneme inventories and more complex phonotactics, would exhibit stronger DLM because speakers compensate for parsing difficulty by compressing dependencies. We test this hypothesis across 55 languages from 10 families using Universal Dependencies treebanks. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and a novel phoneme-space metric, then correlate phonological density with DLM strength. Results show a weak positive correlation (Spearman $r = 0.19$, $p = 0.17$) that fails to reach significance. Phonological density explains only 5% of variance in mean dependency distance ($R^2 = 0.05$). Word-order typology also fails to predict MDD significantly (ANOVA $F = 1.36$, $p = 0.27$). We confirm the functional-lexical split: functional dependencies average 2.7 words while lexical dependencies average 3.4 words. The hypothesis that phonological density constrains syntactic optimization is not supported. We discuss alternative explanations and propose phoneme-space distance as a methodological contribution for future work.\n     6\t\n     7\t## 1. Introduction\n     8\t\n     9\tDependency distance minimization (DLM) is one of the most replicated empirical regularities in cross-linguistic syntax. Since Liu's [1] foundational study of 15 languages, researchers have documented that heads and dependents tend to appear near each other in linear order, reducing working-memory load during parsing [2, 3]. The effect holds across typologically diverse families and both spoken and written modalities [4].\n    10\t\n    11\tTwo blindspots persist in this literature. First, DLM has always been measured in word-space: distance equals the number of intervening words between head and dependent. This ignores phonological structure. A dependency spanning three monosyllabic words in Japanese occupies far less phonological space than the same word-count dependency in English. Second, no study has asked whether phonological density modulates DLM strength. Languages with larger phoneme inventories and more complex syllable structures face harder parsing problems. If speakers compensate by minimizing syntactic distance, phonologically dense languages should show stronger DLM.\n    12\t\n    13\tWe test this phonotactic constraint hypothesis using Universal Dependencies treebanks from 55 languages spanning 10 families. We compute dependency distances in both word-space and phoneme-space, where distance equals the number of phonemes between head and dependent. We then correlate phonological density\u2014derived from PHOIBLE inventory sizes\u2014with mean dependency distance, controlling for word-order typology.\n    14\t\n    15\tThe results do not support the hypothesis. Phonological density shows a weak positive correlation with MDD ($r = 0.19$), running in the opposite direction from prediction. The effect is not statistically significant ($p = 0.17$). Word order similarly fails to predict MDD. The phoneme-space metric produces larger values (mean 10.6 phonemes) than word-space (mean 3.0 words), confirming that phonological and syntactic distances operate on different scales.\n    16\t\n    17\tThese null findings are informative. They suggest that phonological density and syntactic optimization operate independently, or that the hypothesized compensation mechanism is too weak to detect at the cross-linguistic level. We discuss alternative explanations in Section 6.\n    18\t\n    19\tOur contributions are threefold. First, we introduce a phoneme-space dependency distance metric that separates phonological from syntactic proximity. Second, we report the largest cross-linguistic test of the phonotactic constraint hypothesis to date, covering 55 languages. Third, we replicate the functional-lexical dependency split [5] and document family-level variance patterns.\n    20\t\n    21\t## 2. Related Work\n    22\t\n    23\tDependency distance minimization was first documented by Liu [1] for 15 languages, establishing MDD as a universal tendency. Futrell et al. [2] extended this to 37 languages using Universal Dependencies, confirming cross-family robustness. Ferrer-i-Cancho and G\u00f3mez-Rodr\u00edguez [3] showed that reduced dependency distance\u2014the minimum distance over all subsegments\u2014reveals distributed cost structure beyond simple MDD.\n    24\t\n    25\tThe information-theoretic perspective provides a complementary framework. Coup\u00e9 et al. [6] demonstrated that languages encode information at approximately 39 bits per second, with syllable rate and information density compensating for each other. This modulation integrity hypothesis suggests that phonological and syntactic constraints jointly optimize around channel capacity. Our work tests a specific instantiation: whether phonological density modulates syntactic optimization.\n    26\t\n    27\tFunctional versus lexical dependencies form a well-established dichotomy. Gerdes [5] showed that functional dependencies\u2014determiners, case markers, auxiliaries\u2014are universally short because grammar hard-codes their adjacency. Lexical dependencies\u2014subjects, objects, modifiers\u2014are longer and more sensitive to typological variation. Our results confirm this split.\n    28\t\n    29\tCorpus choice affects DLM estimates. Dobrovoljc [7] demonstrated that ordinal rankings of languages by MDD are unstable across treebanks, indicating that corpus composition interacts with distance metrics. Our analysis uses a single standardized treebank per language to minimize this confound, but we acknowledge residual variability.\n    30\t\n    31\tPhonological typology has been linked to syntactic patterns in limited ways. Krielke [8] found diachronic shifts toward intra-phrasal dependencies in scientific German and English, hinting at phonological-density interactions. However, no study has systematically tested whether synchronic phonological density predicts DLM strength.\n    32\t\n    33\t## 3. Method\n    34\t\n    35\t### 3.1 Data\n    36\t\n    37\tWe extracted Universal Dependencies treebanks from the `commul/universal_dependencies` repository on HuggingFace [9]. We selected one configuration per language, preferring treebanks with the largest training splits. This yielded 55 languages across 10 families: Indo-European (28), Uralic (4), Turkic (3), Afroasiatic (4), Sino-Tibetan (4), Austronesian (5), Japonic (1), Koreanic (1), Tai-Kadai (2), and Austroasiatic (2), plus isolates and unclassified entries.\n    38\t\n    39\tPhonological features came from PHOIBLE 2.0 [10]. We used phoneme inventory size as the primary density metric. Phonological density was normalized to $[0, 1]$ by dividing by the maximum observed inventory (70 phonemes). Additional features\u2014phonotactic complexity (max onset + coda cluster size) and syllable type richness\u2014were derived from PHOIBLE but excluded from final models due to missing values for 12 languages.\n    40\t\n    41\tWord-order typology came from WALS [11]. We classified languages as SVO ($n = 41$), SOV ($n = 11$), or VSO ($n = 3$). Register was uniformly written across all treebanks, matching the corpus composition of prior DLM studies [2, 7].\n    42\t\n    43\t### 3.2 Dependency Distance Metrics\n    44\t\n    45\tWord-space MDD follows standard practice [1, 2]. For each dependency arc $(h, d)$, distance equals $|pos(h) - pos(d)|$, the absolute difference in token positions. Mean dependency distance equals the arithmetic mean across all arcs in a treebank.\n    46\t\n    47\tPhoneme-space MDD (PPAD) extends this to phonological units. For each word $w$, we estimated constituent phonemes from PHOIBLE inventory data. We used a family-level average phoneme count as proxy when language-specific G2P was unavailable. Phoneme-space distance for arc $(h, d)$ equals:\n    48\t\n    49\t$$PPAD(h, d) = |pos(h) - pos(d)| \\times \\frac{\\phi_h + \\phi_d}{2}$$\n    50\t\n    51\twhere $\\phi_w$ is the phoneme count of word $w$. This weights each arc by the phonological ma<response clipped><NOTE>Due to the max output limit, only part of this file has been shown to you.
